# The Liturgical Calendar in the Sehling Church Orders

This guide covers the church year in the evangelical church orders of the sixteenth century, as
printed in Emil Sehling's *Die evangelischen Kirchenordnungen des XVI. Jahrhunderts*. It began
as a guide to the ranking of feasts and now covers the whole calendar. It asks:

- **Which days did the orders keep, and why?** What principles decided which feasts stayed, and
  which went?
- **How were the feasts ranked?** What happened to the medieval grades (*summum*, *duplex*,
  *semiduplex*, *simplex*), to octaves, vigils and the transfer of feasts?
- **How was a feast kept in church?** What special rubrics, chants, ceremonies and services did
  the high feasts have, and what was abolished?
- **How was a feast kept in common life?** What rest from work did the orders require, how was
  it enforced, and what became of markets, weddings, guild feasts and the folk festivals of the
  year?
- **How were the seasons kept?** Were there more services in Advent, Lent and Holy Week? What
  became of Rogation week and the Ember days? Which seasons were closed to weddings?
- **What fasts were kept, by whom, where and why?**

**How the guide is laid out.**
- §1 summarizes the findings, and §2 sets out the sources and method.
- §§3–9 deal with the feasts themselves:
  - §3, why feasts were kept at all;
  - §4, the medieval grades;
  - §5, the Lutheran scale of feasts;
  - §6, whole and half holy days;
  - §7, the following days of the high feasts;
  - §8, individual feasts;
  - §9, the *Vierzeiten*.
- §10 covers the ceremonial marks of the feasts in church, the festal ceremonies that were
  abolished, and the seasonal changes in the service.
- §11 covers the keeping of holy days in common life.
- §12 covers the seasons: Advent, Lent, Holy Week, Rogation week, the closed seasons and the
  Ember days.
- §13 covers fasting.
- §14 deals with occurrence and transfer, and §15 with the short calendars of the Upper German
  towns and the Reformed.
- §16 is a concordance of every order quoted.

Each topic is supported by quotations in the original language (Early New High German, Low
German or Latin), each followed by an English translation.

**Conventions**

- **Quotations.** Every quotation is Sehling's text as it stands in the `eko.db` database built
  from the digitized edition.
  - Footnote numbers, sigla, folio markers, the editor's dates in square brackets (such as "[25.
    Dez.]") and interleaved apparatus are left out.
  - Sehling's own supplied letters in square brackets are kept, as are "[!]" and his other
    signs.
  - Spelling and punctuation are as printed. A few plain OCR errors are corrected silently (for
    example "Mariae" for the printed "Marfae").
  - "[…]" marks an omission.
  - Every quotation has been checked against the database text, allowing for OCR noise: at least
    90 per cent of its words must be found there in order. Its page has been checked against the
    edition's page markers.
  - Each quotation is preceded by an HTML comment, invisible when rendered, that names the
    `eko.db` document it was checked against.
- **Translations.** The English is formal-equivalence, in the idiom of the Authorized Version:
  - Word order and clause structure are kept where English allows.
  - Stock terms are rendered the same way throughout. *Fest* is "feast". *Feiertag*, *vyrdach*
    is "holy day". *feiern*, *vyren* is "keep holy (day)". *hohe Feste* are "high feasts".
    *Hauptfeste* are "chief feasts". *fürnembste Feste* are "chiefest feasts". *gemeine Feste*
    are "common feasts". *Aposteltage* are "apostles' days". *Vierzeiten*, *veer tide* are "the
    four times". *Amt*, *ampt* is "the office" (the communion service). *verlegen* is "remove"
    or "lay over". *Historie* is "history". *Fasten*, *vasten* is "Lent" when it names the
    season and "fasting" otherwise. *Quatember*, *Fronfasten* are "the Ember days".
  - Words the translation supplies are in square brackets.
- **Citations.** Citations are in the form "Sehling volume, page".
  - The half-volumes of the edition are written 6/1, 6/2, 7/1, 7/2.1, 7/2.2, 17/1, 17/2, 19/1,
    19/2, 20/1 and 20/2, following the digitized volumes.
  - Each order is named by place, title and date as they appear in Sehling's running heads.
    These sometimes differ from the database's record headings (see §2.3).
- **Traditions.** Most of the orders cited are Lutheran, and they are not marked. Every order of
  another tradition is marked where it is cited, by its name in brackets after the order, as
  "Kurpfalz 1563 (Reformed)". The traditions follow the inventory in
  [`CHURCH_ORDERS_GUIDE.md`](CHURCH_ORDERS_GUIDE.md), §3:
  - **Reformed**: the Swiss, Calvinist and Heidelberg churches, among them the German
    territories that turned Reformed after 1560 and the French and Dutch stranger churches;
  - **Moderate Reformed**: the Bucerian and related orders that stood between the Lutheran and
    the Swiss Reformed: Bucer's Strasbourg and the Upper German cities before the Interim,
    Philip of Hesse's church to 1566, Hermann von Wied's Cologne order, and Colmar after 1578;
  - **Philippist**: the churches that held to Melanchthon's later teaching and his *Corpus
    doctrinae* and refused the Formula of Concord: the joint Hessian synods and orders of
    1568–1598, Bremen 1556–1575, Nassau-Dillenburg 1575 and Anhalt 1590–1594;
  - **Bohemian Brethren**: the Hussite Unity of the Brethren in Greater Poland;
  - **Roman Catholic humanist**: the Erasmian orders of Jülich-Cleves-Berg, which reform the old
    church without leaving it.
  - The mark follows the order cited, not the territory, since a territory could change its
    tradition: Kurpfalz is Lutheran in 1556 and Reformed from 1563, and Anhalt and
    Nassau-Dillenburg turned Reformed in the 1580s and 1590s.
  - The Transylvanian Saxons' own orders, chapters and synods are Lutheran.

## 1. Summary of findings

### 1.1 The main findings

**1. The medieval grades did not survive as grades of feasts.**

- No order ranks a feast as *duplex*, *semiduplex* or *simplex*, and none has a system of Office
  grades (§4.1).
- The old vocabulary survives in fragments:
  - *summum* and *minus summum* as names of Kyrie melodies (Pomerania 1542, Buxtehude 1565,
    Nördlingen 1544, Hof 1592);
  - *summa festa* as a name for the highest feasts (Hadersleben 1528, Tangermünde 1603, Gottorf
    1587);
  - the ferial, dominical and festal forms of the Office in chapters and convents;
  - the adverbs *ferialiter*, *festive* and *solenniter*;
  - *per anticipationem* for moving a feast forward (§§4.2–4.6, 14.2).
- Octaves have gone; the "following days" of the high feasts take their place (§7). So have
  vigil fasts; first vespers on the eve take their place (§4.6).

**2. The ranking is theological.** The orders rank feasts by what they commemorate:

- the feasts of Christ, and above all his birth, resurrection and the sending of the Spirit;
- then the days whose "history is written in the Gospel": the Purification, the Annunciation,
  John the Baptist, the Visitation;
- then the apostles, kept for their example;
- then nothing, for feasts that rest on legend (Corpus Christi, the Holy Cross, the Conception,
  Nativity and Assumption of Mary).

The formula "nicht umme der dage willen, sonder umme der historien willen" (Bugenhagen, §3.3)
states the test. Luther's *Formula missae* of 1523 gives its first form (§3.1).

**3. The rank is kept by civil and ceremonial means.** Most orders do not tell the ranks apart
by the liturgical form of the service. They do so by:

- whether work is forbidden all day, until noon, only during the sermon, or not at all (§6);
- how many sermons are preached (§6.3);
- how many days the feast lasts (§7);
- a set of ceremonial marks (§10): Latin chant, the grade of the Kyrie, proper prefaces,
  sequences, figural music and organ, vestments and elevation, bells, the Athanasian Creed,
  communion, and bans on weddings, markets and guild feasts (§11.3).

**4. The usual scale has three ranks below Sunday** (§5.1):

- the high or chief feasts of Christ;
- a second rank of other Gospel feasts (John the Baptist, the Visitation, Michaelmas, and often
  Purification and Annunciation);
- the apostles' days as half holy days.

Some orders draw two ranks only (Luther, Homberg 1526 (Moderate Reformed), and in effect the
Reformed orders of §15). Others number three classes (Grubenhagen 1544, Regensburg 1567, Hoya
1571, Harlingerland 1573/74). Sayn 1582 has four, the fourth being the abolished feasts (§5.5).
Danzig and Thorn speak of "great" and "small" feasts (§5.6).

**5. The families of orders follow their models.**

- **Saxon and Mecklenburg family** (§5.2). Saxony 1539 and Mecklenburg 1552 set the pattern:
  "hohe heubtfest des herrn Christi" first, then "uber diese furnemsten fest" the apostles,
  John, Visitation and Michaelmas, kept in the forenoon. It was taken up across the north and in
  Pfalz-Zweibrücken.
- **Württemberg family** (§5.3). Holy days are proclaimed by the magistrate. At first there is
  one list, later divided into whole days and forenoon days.
- **Bugenhagen family** (§5.4). "The three great feasts of Christ" are kept three full days
  each. The other Gospel feasts are also kept fully. The apostles are moved to the next Sunday.

**6. Whole and half holy days** (§6). From the 1560s the orders of the Harz, Hesse, the Rhine
and the Main set out day-by-day tables of whole and half days: Mansfeld 1580, Pfalz-Zweibrücken
1561, Landau 1562, Pfalz-Veldenz 1574, Ysenburg 1582, Nassau-Weilburg 1609 and 1618, Solms 1603.
Harvest, markets and craftsmen's work pushed the summer feasts down to half days or onto
Sundays. The Transylvanian synod of 1578 has the technical term: the apostles' days are kept
*intercise*, "by halves" (§6.4).

**7. The middle rank is where the orders differ most** (§8).

- **Michaelmas.** It rose to be one of the four chief feasts in the north, in place of the
  Assumption (§8.5, §9).
- **The Assumption.** It was abolished in most orders. In Franconia and Tecklenburg it was kept
  "for the working country-folk" but preached as the Visitation (§8.3).
- **The Visitation.** It was moved to 15 August in the Nürnberg family (§6.4).
- **Purification and Annunciation.** Everywhere they are treated as feasts of Christ, but their
  rank ranges from whole holy day to half day (§8.1).

**8. The *Vierzeiten*** (§9). In north Germany the top rank had a fiscal name: the four feasts
(Christmas, Easter, Whitsuntide and Michaelmas, formerly the Assumption) on which the
"four-times penny" was paid. The payment made the rank, and the rank carried liturgical marks:

- a sermon at first vespers (Wolfenbüttel 1543);
- a Latin Mass (Hoya 1571);
- proper prefaces (Lippe 1571).

**9. Occurrence and transfer** (§14). The orders keep simple rules of precedence:

- A feast of Christ on a Sunday displaces the Sunday Gospel; an apostle's day yields to it.
- An apostle's day on a Saturday goes to the Sunday.
- The Annunciation in Holy Week is moved in at least six different ways.

Some orders forbid pastors to move feasts on their own authority.

**10. The minimum** (§15). The Upper German cities (Mülhausen 1529, Reformed; Augsburg 1537,
Esslingen 1537 and Strasbourg 1544, Moderate Reformed) and the Reformed territories (Kurpfalz
1563, Nassau-Dillenburg 1582, Anhalt 1599) kept only Sunday and a few feasts of Christ, which
reduced the ranking to Luther's two ranks. Esslingen's return to the apostles' days after the
"blue Mondays" of 1537 shows how unstable that minimum was.

**11. Feasts in church** (§10). Besides the marks of rank, the orders abolished the acted
ceremonies of the medieval year: the Palm Sunday ass, the Good Friday grave, the Easter image,
the Ascension figure, the Whitsun dove, the Christmas cradle, and all processions with holy
water, crosses and the sacrament (§10.10). Some orders kept seasonal changes in the chant: the
plain Kyrie and no *Gloria* in Advent and Lent, the Creed for "Nun bitten" in Advent (§10.11).

**12. Feasts in common life** (§11).
- **Rest in three degrees.** Sunday and the high feasts were kept wholly. The lesser holy days
  were kept until noon, or only during the sermon (§11.1).
- **Enforcement** was by the magistrate: town gates shut on holy-day mornings, fines, taverns
  closed, horses unharnessed (§11.2).
- **Weddings, guild feasts and markets** were moved away from the high feasts (§11.3).
- **The folk festivals** were attacked as "heathen" in mandate after mandate: *Fastnacht*,
  *Kirchweih*, Walpurgis and May, the Whitsun ales, the Midsummer fires and the *Hagelfeier*
  (§11.4).
- **Shrove Sunday** was turned by Bugenhagen into a feast of baptism (§11.5).

**13. The seasons** (§12).
- **Luther** would not show off Lent, Holy Week or Good Friday "with other rites" than the rest
  of the year (§12.1).
- **Lent** kept its name and became a season of preaching:
  - repentance and the Passion;
  - more weekday sermons;
  - a daily catechism at the hour of the old *Salve Regina*;
  - Sunday examinations before the Easter communion.

  There was no daily Lenten Mass (§12.3).
- **Advent** was kept for preaching on the prophecies and the Last Day (§12.2). **Holy Week**
  was kept for the Supper on Maundy Thursday and the Passion on Good Friday (§12.4).
- **The catechism seasons.** Rogation week, Advent, Lent and harvest became Bugenhagen's four
  catechism seasons (§12.5).
- **Closed seasons.** Weddings were still forbidden in Advent and Lent and at the high feasts,
  for a new reason: they drew people from the sermon (§12.6).
- **The Ember days** lost their fast but kept their dates. They became the quarter days for
  payments, catechism series, the Litany, ordinations and the reading of ordinances (§12.7).

**14. Fasting** (§13).
- **The obligation abolished.** The fixed fasts (Lent, Fridays, Ember days, vigils) were
  abolished as obligations binding the conscience. Fasting was still praised as good "to tame
  the body" and was to last "the time of our life" (§13.1).
- **Exhorted, but free.** The earliest orders exhort the people to keep the old fast days freely
  (§13.2).
- **The old fasts kept.** A few, such as Waldeck 1528 and the Catholic Margrave of Baden, kept
  the old fasts (§13.3).
- **Civil abstinence.** Magistrates ordered meat-free days for economic reasons and said so: the
  Nordhausen police order of 1549, which exempts the old, the sick, labourers, children and
  women in childbed (§13.4).
- **The new fasts** were appointed days of fasting and prayer in time of war, plague and danger,
  and fasting before the choice of ministers (§13.5).
- **Before communion.** The one regular fast was the communicant's: the sacrament received
  fasting, and no banquets on the eve of Sundays and feasts (§13.6).

### 1.2 What became of the medieval categories

| Medieval category | In the evangelical orders | § |
|---|---|---|
| *duplex*, *semiduplex*, *simplex*, *totum duplex* | not found as grades of feasts | 4.1 |
| *summum*, *minus summum*, *paschale*, *dominicale*, *angelicum*, *apostolicum*, *feriale* (Kyrie and Gloria melodies) | kept as chant grades in some orders; *summa festa* as the name of the top rank | 4.2, 10.2 |
| *festum fori* / *festum chori* | replaced by whole and half holy days, and by days kept "without a commanded holy day" | 5.2, 6 |
| nine lessons and three lessons | three-lesson matins the norm; nine lessons in some chapters; three lessons for high feasts in villages | 4.3 |
| proper and common of saints | proper Office for high feasts; *commune sanctorum* for saints' days in convents | 4.4 |
| octaves | not kept; forbidden for saints (Ansbach 1533); St Stephen's octave abolished (Mülhausen 1524, Reformed); replaced by the two or three "following days" | 4.4, 7, 15 |
| vigils | vigil fasts abolished; first vespers on the eve, with confession, and on higher feasts a sermon | 4.6 |
| occurrence and transfer | simplified rules of precedence and transfer, including *per anticipationem* | 14 |
| patronal and local feasts | abolished, turned into thanksgivings, or kept with a sermon (Wied 1564, Reformed) | 4.5, 8.9 |

### 1.3 Terms of rank used in the orders

| Term | Meaning in the orders | Examples (§) |
|---|---|---|
| *hohe Feste*, *hohe heubtfest des herrn Christi*, *Hauptfeste* | the highest rank: the feasts of Christ, or the three or four great feasts | Saxony 1539, Mecklenburg 1552 (5.2); Henneberg 1582 (6.3) |
| *de dre groten feste Christi* | Christmas, Easter, Whitsuntide | Braunschweig 1528, Schleswig-Holstein 1542 (5.4) |
| *vier Zeiten*, *veer tide*, *vier hochzeitliche feste*, *Vierzeitenfest* | the four chief feasts on which the four-times penny was paid | Lübeck 1531, Waldeck 1525, Hoya 1571 (9) |
| *summa festa*, *summae festivitates*, *in summis festis* | the highest feasts | Hadersleben 1528, Tangermünde 1603, Gottorf 1587 (3.2) |
| *fürnembste*, *furnemliche*, *vornemlichste Feste* | the chiefest feasts (often the whole list of holy days) | Mecklenburg 1552 (5.2), Württemberg 1553 (4.5), Northeim 1539 (4.5) |
| *andere herliche fest* | the second rank | Grubenhagen 1544 (5.5) |
| *mittelmeßige festa* | middle feasts, kept as half days | Sayn 1582 (5.5) |
| *gemeine Feste*, *gemeine Feiertage* | common feasts: the lower ranks | Pfalz-Zweibrücken 1561 (6.2), Henneberg 1582 (6.3) |
| *große* and *kleine Feste*, *kleine oder halbe feste* | great and small feasts | Danzig 1612, Thorn 1575 (5.6) |
| *ganz*, *halb gefeiert*; *bis Mittag*; *vormittag*; *bis die Sermon geendet* | whole and half holy days | 6 |
| *ohne gebotene Feier* | declared, but without commanded rest | Merseburg 1544 (5.2) |
| *ungefeiret in gedechtnus* | remembered, but not kept holy | Pfalz-Neuburg 1543 (5.7) |
| *solenniter*, *festive*, *festivaliter* | in solemn, festal manner | Schwarzburg 1587 (7.1), Leisnig 1529 (3.5), Pomerania 1569 (10.2) |
| *ferialiter* | (a) in the weekday form, without *Gloria*; (b) as a holiday | Anhalt 1551, 1568; Wied 1564 (Reformed) (3.5) |
| *intercise*, *dimidiatim* | by halves | Mediasch 1578 (6.4) |
| *per anticipationem* | moved back before its date | Wintzingerode (11.2) |
| *de tempore*, *de festo*, *commune sanctorum* | the proper of the season or feast; the common of saints | Mecklenburg 1552 (5.2), Crevese 1541 (3.4) |

## 2. Scope, sources and method

### 2.1 The corpus

The corpus is Sehling's edition, volumes 1–24, as held in `eko.db`. It has 2,350 document
records in 30 digitized volumes and half-volumes, with Sehling's own subject registers linked to
the pages. The edition prints church orders, agendas, visitation instructions and recesses,
parish reports and council decrees. It also prints editorial introductions and notes. This guide
quotes only the sixteenth- and early seventeenth-century texts. The editors' notes are cited as
such where they matter.

### 2.2 How the corpus was searched

Every document was scanned with regular expressions for the groups of terms below. Matches were
read in context, and then the full passages were pulled from the relevant orders.

- **Medieval grades and rubrical terms.**
  - *summum*, *duplex*, *semiduplex*, *simplex*, *totum duplex*, *triplex*, the abbreviations
    *dupl.* and *semidupl.*;
  - *festum fori*, *festum chori*;
  - *novem* / *tres lectiones*;
  - *classis*, *festivitas*, *solemnitas*;
  - *festive*, *solenniter*, *ferialiter*;
  - *commune sanctorum*, *octava*, *vigilia*;
  - *per anticipationem*, *transferre*.
- **German and Low German terms of rank.**
  - *hohe*, *höchste*, *große* and *kleine Feste*, *Hauptfest*, *fürnembste Feste*;
  - *halbe* and *ganze Feiertage*, "bis Mittag", "vormittag";
  - *Aposteltage*, *Marienfeste*, the "third day";
  - *gebannte Feiertage*, *verlegen*, *verschieben*, and their Low German forms (*vyrdage*,
    *hochtide*, *veer tide*).
- **Ceremonial markers.**
  - the *Vierzeiten* and the four-times penny;
  - the Kyrie grades (*summum*, *paschale*, *dominicale*, *angelicum*, *apostolicum*,
    *festivale*, *feriale*, *Fons bonitatis*, *Magnae Deus*) and the *Benedicamus* grades;
  - proper prefaces;
  - bells and chiming (*beiern*).
- **The names of the feasts themselves.** These were searched in lists and in rules for
  transfer.
- **The seasons and fasting** (added for the fuller calendar):
  - *Advent*, *Fasten*, *vasten*, *Quadragesima*, *Marterwoche*, *Gründonnerstag*, *Karfreitag*,
    *Kreuzwoche*, *Bittwoche*, *Hagelfeier*;
  - *Quatember*, *Fronfasten*, *quatertemper*;
  - *fleisch essen*, *Fasttag*, *Bußtag*, *nüchtern*;
  - *Fastnacht*, *vastelavend*, *Aschermittwoch*;
  - *Hochzeit* with *Advent* and *Fasten*.
- **Holy days in common life.** *Sonntag* and *Feiertag* with *arbeit*, *fahren*, *kaufen*,
  *kram*, *zechen*, *tanzen*; *Kirchweih*, *Kirmes*; *Walpurgis*, *Johannisfeuer*, *Meyen*; the
  festal ceremonies (*Palmesel*, *Kindelwiegen*, *Auffahrt*, *Tauben*).

Sehling's subject registers were also used: *Feiertage* (380 page references), *Festtage* and
*Festtage/-Zeiten* (377), *Aposteltage* (135), *Feiertagsordnung* (77), *Vigilien* (95),
*Marienfeste* (27).

**Related guides.**
- [`HYMN_PRACTICE_GUIDE.md`](HYMN_PRACTICE_GUIDE.md) covers the hymns of the seasons and feasts,
  the *Gloria* in Advent and Lent, and the *Leisen* of the high feasts.
- [`PROPERS_GUIDE.md`](PROPERS_GUIDE.md) covers the proper prefaces, sequences and the propers
  of the feasts.
- [`COLLECTS_GUIDE.md`](COLLECTS_GUIDE.md) covers the collects of the seasons and the Lenten
  *super populum* prayers.
- [`OFFICES_GUIDE.md`](OFFICES_GUIDE.md) covers Vespers and the eves of feasts.
- [`SERMONS_GUIDE.md`](SERMONS_GUIDE.md) covers Passion, prayer-day and catechism sermons and
  the duties of hearers.

### 2.3 Cautions

- **Record headings and dates in the database.** A database record often holds more than one
  order, and its year field can be wrong. In this guide every order is named from the running
  head of the page on which the quotation stands. Some examples:
  - The Henneberg record is dated 1555 in the database but holds texts from 1545 to 1582. The
    feast rules are in the *Kirchenordnung* of Georg Ernst of 1582.
  - The Danzig record contains the St Mary's order of 1567, the church fathers' compilation of
    1612, and several Elbing and Thorn texts, among them the Thorn order of 1575.
  - A record among the East Frisian orders, dated 1593 in the database, holds on the pages
    quoted the Harlingerland order of 1573/74.
  - The Sayn record labelled "1314" holds the *Kirchenzuchtordnung* of 1582 and the
    *Kirchenordnung* of 1590.
  - The Mansfeld feast lists belong to the *Kirchen-agenda* of 1580.
  - The Anhalt record holds orders from 1532 to 1572.
- **Editorial summaries and apparatus.** A few texts are quoted from the editors' apparatus, not
  the main text, and are marked as such:
  - Saxony 1539 is printed partly as a summary with quotations.
  - Variants: Nassau-Weilburg's *Agende* of 1618, Brenz's recension B, the German text B of the
    Burzenland articles, and the Latin report of the Mediasch synod.
- **Two-column prints.** Where the edition prints in two columns, the digitized text sometimes
  interleaves them. The Danzig list of 1612 and the Schwarzburg list of 1587 are examples.
  Quotations skip the interleaved matter and mark the gap with "[…]".
- **Silence.** When an order does not mention a feast, it is not always abolished. Many orders
  list only the days that are holy days. Days kept "ungefeiret in gedechtnus", in memory but
  without rest, often go unnamed.

---

## 3. Why keep feasts at all: the principles behind the ranking

Before an order ranks feasts, it usually says why any feast besides Sunday should be kept. Its
answer decides the ranking:

- **The feasts of Christ.** Those that set forth the work of redemption come first.
- **Feasts with a Gospel history.** Feasts whose history is told in the Gospel come next.
- **The apostles and the rest.** The apostles, and other days kept only for their examples, come
  last.
- **Legend and abuse.** Feasts with no ground in Scripture, or bound up with abuse, are
  abolished.

The passages below give the main forms of the argument.

### 3.1 Luther, *Formula missae*, 1523

**Wittenberg, Luther, *Formula missae et communionis*, 1523** (Sehling 1, p. 5):

<!-- doc 2 -->
> Primo, introitus dominicales et in festis Christi, nempe paschatis, pentechostes, nativitatis,
> probamus et servamus, quamquam psalmos mallemus, unde sumpti sunt, ut olim, sed nunc sic usui
> recepto indulgebimus. Quod si qui apostolorum, virginis aliorumque sanctorum introitus (quando
> e psalmis aut aliis scripturis sumpti sunt) probare volent, non damnamus. Nos Wittembergae
> solis dominicis et festis domini sabathissare quaeremus, omnium sanctorum festa prorsus
> abroganda, vel si quid dignum in eis est, in dominicalibus concionibus miscenda esse putamus.
> Festum purificationis et annunciationis pro festis Christi, sicut epiphanian et
> circumcisionem, habemus. Loco festi S. Stephani et Iohannis Evangelistae officium nativitatis
> placet. Festa S. Crucis anathema sunto. Alii faciant pro sua conscientia vel aliorum
> infirmitate, quod spiritus suggesserit.

First, the introits of the Sundays and of the feasts of Christ, namely of Easter, Pentecost and
the Nativity, we approve and keep, although we had rather have the psalms from which they are
taken, as of old; but now we will bear with the received use. And if any will approve the
introits of the apostles, of the Virgin, and of other saints (when they are taken out of the
psalms or other Scriptures), we condemn them not. We at Wittenberg seek to keep sabbath only on
the Lord's days and the feasts of the Lord; the feasts of all the saints we judge are to be
utterly abrogated, or, if there be aught worthy in them, to be mingled into the Sunday sermons.
The feast of the Purification and of the Annunciation we hold for feasts of Christ, as also the
Epiphany and the Circumcision. In the stead of the feast of St Stephen and of John the
Evangelist, the office of the Nativity pleaseth us. The feasts of the Holy Cross, let them be
anathema. Let others do according to their own conscience, or for the infirmity of others, what
the Spirit shall suggest.

**Note.** This is the charter of the Lutheran calendar. It sets out four points:

- **Two ranks only.** Sundays and "feasts of the Lord" are kept, and nothing else.
- **Marian feasts reclassified.** The two Marian feasts with a Gospel history, the Purification
  and the Annunciation, are counted as feasts of Christ.
- **Stephen and John absorbed.** Their days are taken into Christmas.
- **Holy Cross.** The feasts of the Holy Cross are damned outright.

The last sentence leaves room for local custom, and most later orders used it. They kept the
apostles, the Visitation, John the Baptist and Michaelmas at a lower rank.

### 3.2 Homberg, 1526: only the mysteries of redemption

The Homberg synod's *Reformatio* is a Moderate Reformed order of Philip of Hesse's church.


**Hessen, *Reformatio ecclesiarum Hassiae* (Homberg), 1526, cap. 8**
(Moderate Reformed; Sehling 8, p. 49):

<!-- doc 2249 -->
> De festis et commemorationibus cap. 8 [38] Praeter Dominicam diem nullum festum celebretur,
> nisi pro mysteriis nostrae redemptionis. Adeo haec sola de caetero habeantur: Nativitatis
> Christi, Circumcisionis, Epiphaniae, Praesentationis Domini in templum, Incarnationis Verbi
> Dei sive Annuntiationis, diei Parasceves, Paschae die primo, Ascensionis et Pentecostes die
> primo, item dies Visitationis Beatae Mariae. […] Horum quippe celebratio non in factis
> superstitiosis, sed in verbi sancti frequentia et operibus misericordiae est sita.

Of feasts and commemorations, chapter 8. [38] Beside the Lord's day let no feast be celebrated,
save for the mysteries of our redemption. Wherefore let these only be kept henceforth: of the
Nativity of Christ, of the Circumcision, of the Epiphany, of the Presentation of the Lord in the
temple, of the Incarnation of the Word of God or Annunciation, of the day of the Preparation, of
Easter the first day, of the Ascension, and of Pentecost the first day; item the day of the
Visitation of the Blessed Mary. […] For the celebration of these standeth not in superstitious
deeds, but in the frequent use of the holy Word and in works of mercy.

**Note.** This is the strictest one-rank calendar of any Lutheran-leaning order in the corpus:

- **Nothing between Sunday and the feasts of redemption.** There are no apostles, no John the
  Baptist and no Michaelmas.
- **One day each.** Easter and Pentecost are kept for their "first day" only.

The Homberg *Reformatio* was never put into force. The later Hessian orders (also Moderate
Reformed) keep more (§3.6, §6.1).

### 3.3 "Not for the days' sake, but for the histories": Bugenhagen

**Braunschweig, *Kirchenordnung* (Bugenhagen), 1528** (Sehling 6/1, p. 380):

<!-- doc 1983 -->
> Wy sind vor Gade in unsen conscientien an neyne tidt vorbunden, overs sulck schüt deme
> gemeynen volke to denste, dewile alle stucken der hilgen scrift up eyne tidt nicht gehandelt
> konen werden. Darumme holde wy ock de hogen feste Christi unde andere, darvan in deme
> evangelio historien synt bescreven, nicht umme der dage willen, welke gelden (vor Gade unde by
> den rechtvorstendigen Christen) like vele, de eyne so vele alse de andere, sonder umme der
> historien willen to predigen.

We are before God in our consciences bound to no time; but such is done for the service of the
common people, forasmuch as all the parts of holy Scripture cannot be handled at one time.
Therefore we keep also the high feasts of Christ, and others whereof histories are written in
the Gospel, not for the days' sake, which (before God and with Christians of right
understanding) are worth alike, the one as much as the other, but for the histories' sake, to
preach them.

**Note.** The same words stand in Hamburg 1529 (Sehling 5, pp. 513–514) and, in shorter form, in
the other Bugenhagen orders. The test, "whereof histories are written in the Gospel", is the one
most later orders use to rank a feast.

**Schleswig-Holstein, *Kirchenordnung*, 1542** (Sehling 23, p. 95):

<!-- doc 1576 -->
> Van Vyerdagen. Denne wy möthen etlike Feste beholden, nicht um der dage willen, sonder van
> wegen der Predige des Gödtliken wordes, dat alle stücke des Hilligen Evangelii mögen
> bequemlick uthgelecht werden. Wy nemen nene vyerdage an, den allene de gewöntlike Sondage,
> Darmede men van dem arbeide rouwen, dat Word Gades hören, Dat Sacrament entfangen unde ynt
> gemene vor alle nodturfft bidden, ock Gade vor syne woldadt dancken mögen.

Of holy days. For we must retain certain feasts, not for the days' sake, but because of the
preaching of the divine Word, that all the parts of the holy Gospel may be conveniently
expounded. We accept no holy days save only the wonted Sundays, whereon men may rest from
labour, hear the Word of God, receive the Sacrament, and pray in common for every need, and also
thank God for his benefits.

### 3.4 A school timetable of doctrine

**Saxony (Ernestine), *Unterricht der Visitatoren*, 1528** (Sehling 1, p. 164):

<!-- doc 9 -->
> Welche feste also geordnet sind, denn man kann “nicht alle stücke des evangelii einmals leren.
> Darümb man solche lere ins jar geteilet hat. Wie man in einer schule ordnet, auf einen tag
> Virgilium, auf den andern Homerum zu lesen.

Which feasts are so ordained because one cannot teach all the parts of the Gospel at once.
Therefore such doctrine hath been divided through the year, as in a school one ordaineth to read
Virgil on one day and Homer on another.

The Württemberg order has the feasts of Christ follow the order of his acts. **Württemberg,
*Kirchenordnung*, 1536** (Sehling 16, p. 105):

<!-- doc 651 -->
> Haben wir für gut angesehen, das man ein fest nach dem andern begee unnd in den festen Christi
> eben die ordnung halte, die in den thaten selbs, natürlicher ordnung und übernatürlicher
> götlicher anschikkung unnd würckung nach, sich von anfang der empfencknus unnd menschwerdung
> Christi, unsers liebsten heilands, uns zu erlösung unnd ewiger herrlicheyt so gnedigklich
> eines nach dem andern erzeiget hat.

We have thought it good that one feast be kept after another, and that in the feasts of Christ
that very order be held which, in the deeds themselves, according to the natural order and to
the supernatural divine appointment and working, hath shewn itself so graciously one after
another, from the beginning of the conception and incarnation of Christ our dearest Saviour,
unto our redemption and eternal glory.

### 3.5 Not a commandment of God: a civil and pastoral ordinance

**Schwäbisch Hall, Brenz, *Kirchenordnung*, 1527** (Sehling 17/1, p. 53):

<!-- doc 755 -->
> Bey den kristen ist kein eusserlicher, von Got gebotner Feyertag, wie by den Juden der Sabath
> und ander Festen gewesen sein, sonder ist ein stetter, ewiger Sabbath oder Feyertag, vom Tauff
> anhebend, biß in den tod werdendt, darin nimer kein unrecht gescheft volnbracht sol werden.
> Doch in einer cristenlichen versamlung zur eusserlichen noturfft mag ein ordnung in tagen und
> Zeytten mit Feyertagen und wercktagen gehalten werden in zuchtiger ordnung und eusserlicher
> ainikait, nit zu einem gewißen, als von Got gebotten und zur frumckait und selsellikait notig

Among Christians there is no outward holy day commanded of God, as the Sabbath and other feasts
have been among the Jews; but there is a continual, everlasting Sabbath or holy day, beginning
from baptism and lasting unto death, wherein no unrighteous business shall ever be performed.
Yet in a Christian congregation, for outward necessity, an order in days and times, with holy
days and working days, may be kept in seemly order and outward unity; not for the conscience, as
commanded of God and needful to godliness and salvation

**Schwäbisch Hall, Brenz, sermon on the magistrate's ordinance of holy days, 1527**
(Sehling 17/1, p. 78):

<!-- doc 756 -->
> Est autem regia via festos non existimare necessarios esse ad felicitatem eternam, sed ad
> honestam civilem pacem et concordiam ac quietem a labore. Quod enim caret alterna requie,
> durabile non est. Atque hac via incedit mandatum magistratus civilis de festis. Ordinavit enim
> dies festos non ad iusticiam divinam necessarios, sed ad publicam pacem et quietem, ut hec sit
> civilis ordinacio, non papatus supersticio.

Now the royal way is to hold feast days not necessary to eternal felicity, but to honest civil
peace and concord and to rest from labour. For that which lacketh alternate rest is not durable.
And in this way goeth the commandment of the civil magistrate concerning feasts. For he hath
ordained feast days not as necessary to divine righteousness, but for the public peace and
quiet, that this may be a civil ordinance, not a superstition of the papacy.

**Northeim, *Kirchenordnung*, 1539** (Sehling 6/2, p. 934):

<!-- doc 2047 -->
> Wiewol es unnötig ist, viel festtage anzurichten, und auch aus kraft christlicher freiheit
> niemand an solche feirtage gebunden sein sol, wie die epistel zun Colossern am andern bezeugt,
> so hat man dennoch umb der schwachen willen, dieweil das evangelion bey uns noch etlichermasse
> neue ist, etliche furnemliche feste, damit das wort deste statlicher getrieben und gehort
> werde, verordnen müssen.

Although it is needless to set up many feast days, and no man also ought to be bound to such
holy days, by virtue of Christian liberty, as the epistle to the Colossians in the second
chapter witnesseth; yet for the weak's sake, forasmuch as the Gospel is among us yet in some
measure new, certain principal feasts have had to be ordained, that the Word may be the more
worthily set forth and heard.

The Hessian order of 1532 (Moderate Reformed) also justifies the apostles' days by the need for
rest. **Hessen, *Kirchenordnung*, 1532** (Moderate Reformed; Sehling 8, p. 75):

<!-- doc 2252 -->
> Nicht sagen wir, daß dise tage besser oder heiliger seint dan andere, sonder daß solchs des
> gemeinen mans und der ungelerten jugent, dermaßen furgnomen, hohe notturft erfordert, welches
> sonst, durch ihre arbeit verhindert, nicht zeit haben, zum weinigsten die notturftigest stuck
> christelichs glaubens zu leren; uberdas ist auch sonst bruderlich und civile, daß man beide,
> menschen und viehe, zun zeiten ihre reuw lasse.

We say not that these days are better or holier than others, but that the great need of the
common man and of the unlearned youth, taken in this wise, requireth it; who otherwise, hindered
by their labour, have no time to learn at the least the most needful parts of the Christian
faith. Moreover it is also otherwise brotherly and civil that both men and cattle be let have
their rest at times.

The Württemberg preamble of 1553 was copied into the Kurpfalz order of 1556, Pfalz-Zweibrücken
1557, Nördlingen 1579 and others. **Württemberg, *Kirchenordnung*, 1553** (Sehling 16, p. 266):

<!-- doc 671 -->
> Dieweil dann die ordnung der Feyertag gmeiner Kirchen dahin dienstlich, das sie bestimpte zeit
> wisse, die Predig und die außtheilung der Heiligen Sacrament zu besuchen, und die gemeinen,
> weltlichen Recht zubeweisung ires gehorsamen diensts gegen Christo, dem Son Gottes, und seiner
> Kirchen ire besondere freiheit den fürnembsten Feirtagen geben, So wöllen wir, das hie
> nachbenannte tag zu Feirtag verkündiget und Christlich gebür nach gehalten werden

Forasmuch then as the order of holy days serveth the common Church to this end, that it may know
an appointed time to resort to the sermon and to the ministering of the holy Sacraments; and the
common temporal laws, to shew their obedient service toward Christ the Son of God and his
Church, give their special freedom to the chiefest holy days: we will that the days hereafter
named be proclaimed for holy days, and kept according to Christian duty

### 3.6 "The feast of all feasts": Hessen, 1566

**Hessen, *Kirchenordnung*, 1566** (Moderate Reformed; Sehling 8, pp. 257–258):

<!-- doc 2257 -->
> haben wir uns auch fürgenommen, etliche festa Christi zu halten. […] Chrysostomus […] in einer
> predig vom heiligen Philogonio erzelet etlich festa Christi und nennet die menschwerdung und
> geburt Christi das fest aller festen. Denn aus diesem haben ihren grund und ursprung
> Epiphania, Ostern, Himmelfart und Pfingsten.

we also have purposed to keep certain feasts of Christ. […] Chrysostom […] in a sermon on the
holy Philogonius rehearseth certain feasts of Christ, and calleth the incarnation and birth of
Christ the feast of all feasts. For out of this have Epiphany, Easter, the Ascension and
Pentecost their ground and origin.

**Note.** This is the only place in the corpus where one feast is ranked above all the others,
and it is done by patristic argument. The order does not draw a liturgical consequence. Its
feasts of Christ are kept "in the form of the Sunday", with only the chants, lessons and prayers
changed (p. 258).

### 3.7 The test of Scripture history

**Saxony (Albertine), *Kirchenordnung* of Duke Heinrich, 1539** (Sehling 1, p. 274):

<!-- doc 30 -->
> Uber die gemeinen sontage sollen gehalten werden die hohen heuptfeste des herrn Christi,
> welche von alters her in der christenheit darzu geordnet sind, das die sondern stücke der
> historien oder geschicht von Christo, so man im glauben bekennet, in gedechtnis des gemeinen
> volks behalten und daran dieselben artikel des christlichen glaubens gehandelt werden. Zu
> solchen festen Christi gehören auch etliche von denen, so man bisher der jungfrauen Marie
> gefeiert hat.

Over and above the common Sundays shall be kept the high chief feasts of the Lord Christ, which
from of old in Christendom were ordained to this end: that the several parts of the history or
story of Christ which are confessed in the Creed might be kept in the remembrance of the common
people, and those same articles of the Christian faith handled thereon. To such feasts of Christ
belong also some of those that were hitherto kept for the Virgin Mary.

**Note.** This paragraph, the list that follows it (§5.2) and the rule for the apostles' days
became the model for the Mecklenburg order of 1552. Through that order it reached the Brunswick,
Lüneburg, Oldenburg, Lippe, Verden and Palatinate-Zweibrücken orders.

Pfalz-Neuburg uses the same test to decide which days break the continuous reading of Scripture.
**Pfalz-Neuburg, *Kirchenordnung*, 1543** (Sehling 13, p. 71):

<!-- doc 386 -->
> Doch ausgenommen die hohen fest, die ir historien, darumb sie aufgesetzt sein, in der heiligen
> schrift haben als Weihennacht, Obersten, Lichtmeß, Annunciationis, Palmtag, Ostern,
> Auffartstag, Pfingstag, Johannis des taufers, Visitationis etc.; dann an disen festen sol man
> die capitel aus der bibel lesen, für die epistel und evangelia, darin die historia des fests
> beschrieben und die sonst darzu verordnet sein.

Yet excepted the high feasts which have their histories, for the sake whereof they were set up,
in holy Scripture, as Christmas, Twelfth Day, Candlemas, the Annunciation, Palm Sunday, Easter,
Ascension Day, Whitsunday, John the Baptist, the Visitation, etc.; for on these feasts one shall
read, for the epistle and Gospel, the chapters out of the Bible wherein the history of the feast
is written and which are otherwise appointed thereto.

**Gera, *Kirchen-Ordnung*, 1556**
(Sehling 2, p. 158; an extract of the order of the Vogtland superintendents made at Plauen in 1552):

<!-- doc 1234 -->
> III. Festa und feiertage. Neben den gewönlichen sonntag sollen die feste Christi und der
> heiligen apostel, so evangelia und historien in der schrift geben, in allen gemeinen feierlich
> gehalten, daran gottes wort mit vleis gepredigt, auch das göttlich ampt mit reichung der
> sacrament, do communicanten vorhanden, und sonderlich der catechismus nachmittage stets
> getrieben werden. Es sollen aber damit diese nachvolgende und keine andere der heiligen oder
> sonsten papistische feste gemeint sein.

III. Feasts and holy days. Beside the wonted Sunday, the feasts of Christ and of the holy
apostles which give Gospels and histories in the Scripture shall be solemnly kept in all
congregations; and on them God's Word shall be diligently preached, and the divine office also
held with the ministering of the sacraments, where there be communicants, and in especial the
catechism be always taught in the afternoon. But hereby shall be meant these following, and no
other feasts of the saints or otherwise papistical.

**Transylvania, Honterus, *Reformationsbüchlein*, 1543** (Sehling 24, p. 189; Latin):

<!-- doc 1666 -->
> Dies festos propter multiplices abusus in angustum contraximus observatis tamen omnibus iis,
> qui in ecclesia generali ob historias in sacris literis comprehensas sunt recepti.

The feast days, because of manifold abuses, we have drawn into a narrow compass, yet keeping all
those which have been received in the general Church for the histories contained in the holy
writings.

**Kosd and Schelken chapters (Transylvania), statutes, 1577** (Sehling 24, p. 400):

<!-- doc 1704 -->
> Nolumus omnino in nostris ecclesiis ullam esse celebrandorum festorum varietatem, sed
> cathalogus festorum dierum in Reformatione Coronensis Ecclesiae traditus observetur. Qui vero
> in eo adscripti non sunt (ut puta in sacris nullam habentes historiam) omittantur.

We will in no wise that there be in our churches any variety in the feasts to be celebrated, but
let the catalogue of feast days delivered in the Reformation of the church of Kronstadt be
observed. But those that are not written therein (as having, namely, no history in holy writ)
let them be omitted.

The Riga order gives a reason of this kind for keeping the Visitation. **Riga, *Kirchenordnung*,
1530** (Sehling 5, p. 17):

<!-- doc 1897 -->
> Alle festa Christi unsers herrn und erlösers, woll wir halten, auf das man das gedechtnus der
> heilsamen und grossen gnadenreichen werck, die uns zu trost geschehen sind, mit predigten und
> ermanung des volcks jerlich begehe, als nemlichen weinachten, circumcisionis, epiphanie,
> purificationis, annunciationis, ostern, pfingsten, mit den andern folgenden tagen, auch
> ascensionis Christi, und visitationis, die weil das die erste offenbarung Christi ist gewesen,
> da er noch in mutter leib war

All the feasts of Christ our Lord and Redeemer will we keep, that the remembrance of the
wholesome and great gracious works which were done for our comfort may be kept yearly with
sermons and exhortation of the people; namely Christmas, the Circumcision, the Epiphany, the
Purification, the Annunciation, Easter, Pentecost, with the other days following, also the
Ascension of Christ, and the Visitation, forasmuch as that was the first revelation of Christ,
when he was yet in his mother's womb

At Regensburg the feasts that the town's Catholics still keep are dropped, because they lack a
Scripture history. **Regensburg, *Kirchenordnung*, 1567** (Sehling 13, p. 474):

<!-- doc 450 -->
> Und wiewol in unser stat die papisten viel mehr feirn halten, als Georgii, Corporis Christi,
> Mariae Magdalenae, Laurentii, Assumtionis Mariae, Nativitatis Mariae, Omnium sanctorum,
> Martini, Catharinae, Nicolai, so haben wir sie doch zu feiern entlich faren lassen und mit
> vorgehender, genugsamer erinnerung an die christlich gemein, wie solche fest nit allein gar
> kein historien heiliger schrift haben, sondern als ganz bäpstisch mit höchster abgötterei und
> zu bestetigung derselben, wider die reine lere unser kirchen vom widerteil gebraucht werden.

And although in our city the papists keep many more holy days, as of George, of Corpus Christi,
of Mary Magdalene, of Laurence, of the Assumption of Mary, of the Nativity of Mary, of All
Saints, of Martin, of Catherine, of Nicholas, yet we have at last let go the keeping of them,
with foregoing sufficient admonition to the Christian congregation, how such feasts not only
have no history of holy Scripture at all, but are used by the adverse part, as wholly
papistical, with the highest idolatry and to the confirming of the same, against the pure
doctrine of our churches.

### 3.8 The Sabbath argument: Sunday first in dignity

Most Lutheran orders treat the choice of days as free. The synodal order of the Bohemian
Brethren (Hussite), printed among the Posen texts, grounds Sunday's precedence in the moral law.
**Posen, Bohemian Brethren, synodal order, 1576** (Bohemian Brethren; Sehling 4, p. 285):

<!-- doc 1846 -->
> VII. Consuetudo circa dies festos. Inter dies festos primam tribuimus dignitatem septimo diei,
> dominico, quem arbitramur nemini hominum ad operas externas et serviles, ullo etiam libertatis
> christianae praetextu adhibere licere. ldque propterea, quod institutionem eius non pro
> ceremonia, sed pro parte moralis, aeternum inviolabilis, legis agnoscimus.

VII. The custom concerning feast days. Among the feast days we give the first dignity to the
seventh day, the Lord's, which we judge it lawful for no man to employ in outward and servile
works, under any pretext even of Christian liberty; and that because we acknowledge its
institution not for a ceremony, but for a part of the moral law, everlastingly inviolable.

---

## 4. The medieval grades and what became of them

The late-medieval rite ranked a feast in two ways. The first was its liturgical grade: *totum
duplex*, *duplex*, *semiduplex* and *simplex*, with *summum* or *duplex maius* at the top in
many diocesan uses. The second was its civil status: a *festum fori*, on which work was
forbidden, or a *festum chori*, kept only in choir. The grade decided the Office: nine lessons
or three, proper or common, first vespers, an octave, the colour and the ceremony. This section
asks which of these terms and categories the evangelical orders kept.

### 4.1 *Duplex*, *semiduplex*, *simplex*: absent

No order in the corpus uses *duplex*, *semiduplex*, *simplex* or *totum duplex* as the grade of
a feast. Every hit for these words is ordinary Latin. The Bohemian Brethren's order for Posen
(1576, Hussite) calls certain duties *duplicia*, and the Gottorf catechism (1571) teaches a
*triplex usus legis*. Neither has anything to do with feasts. There are no abbreviations such as
*dupl.* or *semidupl.* either. *Festum fori* occurs only in a modern editor's note (on Nürnberg
1522). The medieval scale of Office grades did not pass into the evangelical church orders.

### 4.2 *Summum* and *minus summum*: grades of the chant

*Summum* survives, but as the name of a melody, not of a feast. In the late-medieval Kyriale the
Kyrie and Gloria were sung to melodies graded by rank:

- *summum* for the highest feasts, *minus summum* for lesser doubles;
- *paschale* for Eastertide, *dominicale* for Sundays;
- *angelicum*, *apostolicum*, *de martyribus*, *de confessoribus*, *de virginibus* for saints'
  days;
- *feriale* for weekdays.

A number of Lutheran orders keep this system, and with it keep grading the day.

The Pomeranian order gives the grade of the Kyrie at Mass. **Pomerania, *Kirchenordnung*, 1542**
(Sehling 4, p. 356):

<!-- doc 1859 -->
> Darup dat kyrie dominicale, efft me mach ock up die sondage, kyrie angelicum, de martiribus,
> effte confessoribus, efft virginibus, wo man sie genomet hefft singen, dominicale im advent
> und in der vasten, dat paschale tuschen pingsten und paschen, dat summum up die hohern fest,
> effte dat minus summum.

Thereupon the *Kyrie dominicale*; or one may also sing upon the Sundays the *Kyrie angelicum*,
*de martyribus*, or *confessoribus*, or *virginibus*, as they have been named; the *dominicale*
in Advent and in Lent, the *paschale* between Whitsuntide and Easter, the *summum* upon the
higher feasts, or else the *minus summum*.

**Note.** This is the clearest statement in the corpus of the old Kyrie grades applied by rank.
The saints' melodies are now simply alternatives for ordinary Sundays. The *summum* is kept for
"the higher feasts" (*die hohern fest*), and *minus summum* is offered as a lesser alternative
there.

The Buxtehude *Agende* has these headings over its printed Kyrie melodies, which the editor
traces to Lucas Lossius's *Psalmodia*. **Buxtehude, *Agende*, 1565** (Sehling 7/1, p. 123):

<!-- doc 2082 -->
> In summis festivitatibus et dominica Trinitatis cum textu. Minus summum. I. In festis beatae
> virginis Mariae. II. Kyrie paschale. Dominicale. In Adventu Domini. Angelicum Kyrie. Kyrie
> apostolicum. Dominicale.

On the highest festivities and on the Sunday of the Trinity, with the text. *Minus summum*. I.
On the feasts of the blessed Virgin Mary. II. *Kyrie paschale*. *Dominicale*. In the Advent of
the Lord. *Kyrie angelicum*. *Kyrie apostolicum*. *Dominicale*.

At Hof the grades appear in the Latin school Mass in the monastery church. **Hof, *Ordo
ecclesiasticus*, 1592** (Sehling 11, p. 472):

<!-- doc 294 -->
> Mox II. ex missali magno canitur Kyrie certi temporis vel angelicum in Adventu Domini vel
> Kyrie Magnae Deus potentiae de Nativitate Christi, cui Et in terra angelicum subiicitur, vel
> Kyrie dominicale maius post dominicam Septuagesimae usque ad Pascha vel Kyrie paschale vel
> Kyrie Fons bonitatis cum Et in terra angelico vel Kyrie dominicale minus post festum
> Trinitatis vel de apostolis, martyribus etc., prout tempus requirit et libri ordo monstrat.

Straightway, secondly, is sung out of the great missal the Kyrie of the proper season: either
the *angelicum* in the Advent of the Lord; or the Kyrie *Magnae Deus potentiae* of the Nativity
of Christ, whereunto the *Et in terra angelicum* is joined; or the greater *dominicale* after
the Sunday of Septuagesima until Easter; or the Kyrie *paschale*; or the Kyrie *Fons bonitatis*
with the *Et in terra angelicum*; or the lesser *dominicale* after the feast of the Trinity; or
that *de apostolis*, *de martyribus*, etc., as the time requireth and the order of the book
sheweth.

**Note.** At Hof the melodies are chiefly seasonal. Its German *Kirchenamt* still names them by
rank all the same:

- The same Kyrie is called *angelicum seu feriale* in Advent (p. 432).
- It is called *angelicum seu festivale* on St Laurence's day (p. 445) and on the feasts of Mary
  (p. 450).
- The *Benedicamus* at vespers is labelled *festivale*, *dominicale*, *paschale* or *feriale*
  (for example "Benedicamus festivale seu feriale", p. 422).

Nördlingen reserves the *summum* for Pentecost and Trinity. **Nördlingen, *Ordnung der
ceremonien*, 1544** (Sehling 12, pp. 321–322):

<!-- doc 373 -->
> Zu Pfingsten. 1. Introitus de Spiritu oder Kom, hailiger Gaist. 2. Kyrie summum und Et in
> terra wie zu Ostern. […] De sancta Trinitate. 1. Introitus de tempore. 2. Kyrie summum mit dem
> Et in terra. 3. Nach der epistel, den sequenz de tempore.

At Whitsuntide. 1. The introit *de Spiritu*, or *Kom, hailiger Gaist*. 2. *Kyrie summum* and *Et
in terra*, as at Easter. […] Of the holy Trinity. 1. The introit *de tempore*. 2. *Kyrie summum*
with the *Et in terra*. 3. After the epistle, the sequence *de tempore*.

In Mecklenburg the grade name passes to a German Kyrie. **Mecklenburg, *Ordeninge der misse*,
1545** (Sehling 5, p. 151):

<!-- doc 1922 -->
> Das düdesche kyrie summum: Ach vader, alder högeste godt, schölen de kerckheren up den dörpern
> singen, unde dem volke idt leren, dat de ganze kercke idt singe.

The Dutch [that is, German] *Kyrie summum*, "Ah Father, God most high of all", shall the parsons
in the villages sing, and teach it the people, that the whole church may sing it.

**"The highest feasts" as a category.** Outside the chant books, *summa festa* or *summae
festivitates* is used simply as the name of the top rank of days.

The Hadersleben article on Latin singing. **Hadersleben, *Artikel*, 1528** (Sehling 23, p. 64):

<!-- doc 1576 -->
> Eth schal ock friig sin, dat sie in den groten festen mögen Gloria in excelsis up latin
> singen, des gelicken Alleluya und eine sequentien, alse Grates nunc omnes, Victime Paschali,
> Veni Sancte Spiritus, und de leyssen darinn, item dat latinische Patrem, prefatien, Sanctus
> unnd Agnus Dei, doch dat aleine up di groten feste.

It shall also be free that they may on the great feasts sing *Gloria in excelsis* in Latin,
likewise the Alleluia and a sequence, as *Grates nunc omnes*, *Victimae paschali*, *Veni Sancte
Spiritus*, and the *Leisen* therein; item the Latin *Patrem*, the prefaces, the Sanctus and the
*Agnus Dei*: yet that only upon the great feasts.

**Note.** In the Latin version of these articles, preserved in the edition's apparatus, this
article is headed *De Latine cantionibus in summis festis*. The Low German "groten feste" and
the Latin *summa festa* are the same rank.

Tangermünde's *Ritus* is a local custom-book, printed from Küster. **Tangermünde, *Ritus*,
1603** (Sehling 3, p. 338):

<!-- doc 1788 -->
> Si summae festivitates celebrando veniunt et incipiuntur, a die solis per totam septimanam a
> concionandi labore feriantur ministri. 3. In summis festis diaconus, qui matutinam habet
> concionem, explicat textum evangelicum, pastor alium convenientem. Alter diaconus lectionem
> Epistolae.

If the highest festivities come to be celebrated and are begun, the ministers shall rest from
the labour of preaching from the Sunday through the whole week. 3. On the highest feasts the
deacon that hath the morning sermon expoundeth the text of the Gospel, the pastor another that
is convenient, the other deacon the lesson of the Epistle.

**Gottorf, visitation articles, 1587** (Sehling 23, p. 371):

<!-- doc 1621 -->
> 12. An Praefatio Dominus vobiscum in summis festis ante communionem cantetur et qua lingua.

12. Whether the Preface, *Dominus vobiscum*, be sung on the highest feasts before the communion,
    and in what tongue.

### 4.3 Nine lessons and three

In the medieval Office, a feast of higher grade had a matins of three nocturns and nine lessons.
A simple feast or a feria had one nocturn and three lessons. Luther's *Formula missae* already
takes the three-lesson matins as the norm for ordinary days.

**Wittenberg, Luther, *Formula missae*, 1523** (Sehling 1, p. 9):

<!-- doc 2 -->
> In reliquis diebus, quas ferias vocamus, nihil video, quod non ferri possit, modo missae
> abrogentur. Nam matutinae trium lectionum et horae, tum vesperae et completorium de tempore
> (exclusis sanctorum feriis) nihil sunt nisi scripturae divinae verba.

In the rest of the days, which we call ferias, I see nothing that may not be borne, so that the
masses be abolished. For the matins of three lessons, and the hours, and also vespers and
compline *de tempore* (the holy days of the saints being excepted), are nothing but the words of
divine Scripture.

Pfalz-Neuburg still reckons with both forms in chapters and monasteries. **Pfalz-Neuburg,
*Kirchenordnung*, 1543** (Sehling 13, pp. 77–78 and 95):

<!-- doc 386 -->
> Die mögen in den stiften und clöstern, da man teglich metten singt, also geordnet werden, das,
> wann man novem lectiones helt, drei aus den historischen büchern der bibel als Mose, Josua,
> Judicum, Ruth, Samuel, der Könige und Chronica, […] darnach drei aus den propheten […]
> Desgleichen sollen die drei metten in der marterwochen zu ir gewonlichen zeit auch nach
> obgemeltem unterricht, doch on alles klopfen, rumpeln und getümmel gehalten werden, allein
> das, wo man sie novem lectionum helt, sollen die letsten drei oder, wo man sie allein trium
> lectionum helt, sollen dieselben drei in den pfarrkirchen und, wo das, gemein volk darbei ist,
> teutsch gelesen werden

These may be so ordered in the chapters and cloisters where matins is sung daily, that when nine
lessons are kept, three be taken out of the historical books of the Bible, as Moses, Joshua,
Judges, Ruth, Samuel, the Kings and Chronicles, […] thereafter three out of the prophets […]
Likewise the three matins in the week of the Passion shall be kept at their wonted time, and
also according to the instruction aforesaid, yet without all knocking, rumbling and tumult; only
that where they are kept with nine lessons, the last three, or where they are kept with three
lessons only, those same three, shall be read in German in the parish churches, and where the
common people are present.

In Waldeck a high feast raises the village matins by one grade. **Waldeck, *Kirchenordnung*,
1556** (Sehling 9, p. 276):

<!-- doc 2300 -->
> Frü zur Metten Sollen die Schüler mit dem Veni sancte anfangen. Volgents eyn Invitatorium und
> Venite, ob man kan, Darnach Antiphon und Psalmen de Dominica oder Festo, Hierauff die
> Lectiones und Responsoria des gemeynen Sontags und heyligentages, wie droben unter dem titel:
> Kirchen Ordnung auff den Dörffern, Aber auff hohe Festtage drei Lectiones und Responsoria, dem
> fest gemeß, Volgents Te Deum laudamus, Benedictus cum sua Antiphona, Collecten und Benedicamus
> singen.

Early, at matins, the scholars shall begin with the *Veni sancte*; next an invitatory and
*Venite*, if it can be done; thereafter antiphon and psalms *de Dominica* or *de Festo*;
hereupon the lessons and responds of the common Sunday and holy day, as above under the title
"Church order in the villages"; but upon high feast days three lessons and responds, agreeable
to the feast; next sing *Te Deum laudamus*, *Benedictus* with its antiphon, the collects and
*Benedicamus*.

In the Transylvanian order the more solemn feasts keep a fuller matins. **Transylvanian Saxons,
*Kirchenordnung*, 1547** (Sehling 24, p. 222; Latin version):

<!-- doc 1669 -->
> In solennioribus autem festis tres antiphonae cum suis psalmis retinentur. […] His succedit:
> Te Deum laudamus Germanicum, in maioribus festis etiam Latinum.

But on the more solemn feasts three antiphons with their psalms are retained. […] These are
followed by the *Te Deum laudamus* in German, on the greater feasts in Latin also.

### 4.4 Proper, common and *de tempore*

Where the Office was still sung in a chapter or convent, the orders use the old categories of
the Office. These are the ferial, dominical and festal forms; the proper *de festo* against the
*commune sanctorum*; the octave; and the *suffragia*.

**Goslar, collegiate church of SS. Simon and Jude, *Ordnung für das Stundengebet*, 1534**
(Sehling 7/2.2, p. 264):

<!-- doc 2194 -->
> Vesper Psalmi feriales cum una Gloria et una anthifona, dar nha lectio ex Novo testamento,
> inchoande in principio Mathei etc., in festo responsorium de festo, imnus ferialis aut
> dominicalis vel de festo, Magnificat cum anthifona feriali, in Dominica tertia anthifona ex
> ewangelio dominicali, in festivitatibus de festo, dar nach Kyrieleyson - Christeleyson -
> Kyrieleyson, Pater noster, versiculus Ostende nobis, Domine, misericordiam tuam etc., collecta
> dominicalis vel festivalis, Benedicamus.

Vespers. The ferial psalms with one *Gloria* and one antiphon; thereafter a lesson out of the
New Testament, beginning at the beginning of Matthew, etc.; on a feast the respond of the feast;
the hymn ferial, or dominical, or of the feast; *Magnificat* with the ferial antiphon, on Sunday
the third antiphon out of the Sunday Gospel, on festivities of the feast; thereafter *Kyrie
eleison, Christe eleison, Kyrie eleison*, *Pater noster*, the versicle *Ostende nobis, Domine,
misericordiam tuam*, etc., the dominical or festal collect, *Benedicamus*.

**Crevese, Benedictine nunnery, visitation recess, 1541** (Sehling 3, p. 203):

<!-- doc 1762 -->
> Sunst sollen ock die junkfrowen gemeinlich horas de tempore, und van hohen festen, wa bether
> gescheen, halden. Desgleichen, wan ein fest de sanctis kommet, sollen sie dat commune
> sanctorum halden und duse gesenge alle, wie in dem stift to Coln an der Sprew gesungen werd,
> reformert.

Otherwise also the maidens shall commonly keep the hours *de tempore*, and of the high feasts,
as hitherto hath been done. Likewise, when a feast *de sanctis* cometh, they shall keep the
*commune sanctorum*, and all these chants reformed, as they are sung in the chapter at Cölln on
the Spree.

**Note.** Proper Offices are allowed for the high feasts. A saint's day gets only the common
Office, which names no saint's legend. The same wording recurs in the other Brandenburg convent
recesses (Sehling 3, pp. 219, 238, 272, 305).

At the Ansbach chapters saints' octaves and suffrages are forbidden. **Brandenburg-Ansbach,
*Ordnung singens und lesens bei den Stiften*, 1533** (Sehling 11, p. 314):

<!-- doc 276 -->
> Alle suffragia de sanctis sollen sie underlassen; dann sie stellen das vertrauen in die
> hailigen wider das erst gebot Gottes. Allein mogen sie an den werktagen suffragia pro pace und
> pro peccatis halten. Item sie sollen auch von kainem hailigen die octava, den achtenden,
> halten. Die histori Trinitatis ist rain; die mögen sie halten, als sie wollen.

All suffrages *de sanctis* they shall leave off, for they set trust in the saints against the
first commandment of God. Only upon the working days they may keep the suffrages *pro pace* and
*pro peccatis*. Item, they shall also keep the octave, the eighth day, of no saint. The history
of the Trinity is pure; that they may keep as they will.

**Note.** This is the only explicit ruling on octaves among the rank passages. Elsewhere octaves
are never forbidden, but they are also simply never mentioned. The Lutheran "three days" of the
high feasts (§7) took their place.

### 4.5 *Ferialiter*, *festive*, *solenniter*

Three adverbs from the old rubrics survive: *ferialiter*, *festive* and *solenniter*.
*Ferialiter* is used in two opposite senses:

- In Anhalt it keeps its medieval meaning: "in the weekday manner", without the *Gloria in
  excelsis*.
- In the county of Wied (Reformed) it means "as a holiday", from classical *feriae*.

Anhalt's order of the German hymns builds a three-step scale into the Mass:

- the high feasts, with sequence, *Patrem*, preface, Sanctus and *Agnus Dei*;
- other Sundays and holy days, with German hymns and Luther's exhortation;
- weekdays, *ferialiter*.

**Anhalt, *Ordnung der deutschen Gesänge*, before February 1551** (Sehling 2, pp. 554–555):

<!-- doc 1262 -->
> an hohen festen das alleluia und sequenz, an andern sontagen ader feiertagen ein deutscher
> gesang […] und volgents an hohen festen singe der priester die praefacio und die schueler das
> sanctus. An andern son- und feiertagen soll gelesen werden die vermanunge doctor Mart. Luthers
> an die communicanten. […] Under der communion aber an hohen festen soll gesungen werden das
> agnus dei […] An den werkeltagen soll es also gehalten werden. Fruehe soll eine kurze metten
> gesungen werden; so aber communicanten vorhanden, mag solche metten underlassen, und das ganze
> officium coenae in gleicher mass und gestalt, wie oben angezeigt, doch ferialiter ut vocant
> gehalten werden, als nemblich introitus dominicalis ader ein deutscher psalm kirieleison, doch
> ane gloria in excelsis

on high feasts the Alleluia and sequence, on other Sundays or holy days a German hymn […] and
then on high feasts let the priest sing the preface and the scholars the Sanctus. On other
Sundays and holy days shall be read Doctor Martin Luther's exhortation to the communicants. […]
But under the communion on high feasts shall be sung the *Agnus Dei* […] On the working days it
shall be kept thus. In the morning a short matins shall be sung; but if there be communicants,
such matins may be left off, and the whole office of the Supper kept in like measure and form as
above shewed, yet *ferialiter*, as they call it, namely the Sunday introit or a German psalm,
Kyrie eleison, but without *Gloria in excelsis*

**Anhalt, *Kirchenordnung* of Prince Bernhard, 1568** (Sehling 2, p. 569):

<!-- doc 1262 -->
> Do aber festum annuntiationis in der marterwoche gefiele, sol mans ufn palmabend halten,
> visitationis und nativitatis Marie ferialiter mit den gewonlichen evangelien zu halten.

But if the feast of the Annunciation fall in the week of the Passion, it shall be kept on the
eve of Palm Sunday; the Visitation and the Nativity of Mary are to be kept *ferialiter*, with
their wonted Gospels.

**Note.** The Visitation and Nativity of Mary are not holy days. Their Gospels are read on the
day, in the weekday form. This is the only order in the corpus that keeps the Nativity of Mary
in this reduced way. Compare §8.4.

Wied (Reformed) has a two-column list of feasts. **Wied, *Synodalbeschlüsse*, 1564**
(Reformed; Sehling 19/1, p. 467):

<!-- doc 1094 -->
> Sequitur catalogus festorum. ferialiter: Circumcisionis Christi Trium Regum Purificationis
> Mariae Annunciationis Mariae Matthiae Visitationis Mariae Pasche cum sequente proxima
> Ascensionis Christi Pentecostes cum sequente Iohannis Baptistae […] Festum Nativitatis Christi
> cum duobus sequentibus diebus Omnium Apostolorum tantum fiet concio: Omnium Sanctorum
> Conversionis Pauli Marci Mariae Magdalenae Laurentii Natalis Mariae Decollationis Iohannis
> Michaelis Catarinae In festis patronorum cuiuslibet Ecclesiae

There followeth the catalogue of feasts. *Ferialiter* [that is, to be kept as holidays]: of the
Circumcision of Christ; of the Three Kings; of the Purification of Mary; of the Annunciation of
Mary; of Matthias; of the Visitation of Mary; of Easter with the next day following; of the
Ascension of Christ; of Pentecost with the day following; of John the Baptist […] the feast of
the Nativity of Christ with the two days following; of all the Apostles. Only a sermon shall be
held: on All Saints; the Conversion of Paul; Mark; Mary Magdalene; Laurence; the Nativity of
Mary; the Beheading of John; Michael; Catherine; on the feasts of the patrons of every church.

**Note.** Sehling's editor reads the first column as the whole holy days and the second as the
half holy days, kept by a morning sermon only. Two things stand out:

- **Michaelmas.** In the north it is one of the four chief feasts (§9). Here it is only a sermon
  day.
- **Patronal feasts.** The feast of each church's patron survives at the lowest grade. No other
  order in the corpus keeps it.

**Amt Leisnig, *Verordnung der Visitatoren*, 1529** (Sehling 1, p. 610):

<!-- doc 105 -->
> Auch sollen die drei hohen feste, allwege drei tag feierlich und festive mit allen cerimonien
> gehalden werden, und sich sunst in allem andern des buch underricht der visitatorn
> gehorsamlich halden

Also the three high feasts shall always be kept three days, solemnly and *festive*, with all
ceremonies; and in all else they shall keep themselves obediently to the book, the Instruction
of the Visitors.

### 4.6 Vigils and first vespers

The medieval vigil, with its fast and vigil office, disappears. What survives is the evening
before a feast: first vespers, with confession for the next day's communicants and on the higher
feasts a sermon. The name *vigilia* survives with it. So does the old rule that only feasts of
some rank have a proper eve.

**Hof, *Ordo ecclesiasticus*, 1592** (Sehling 11, p. 411):

<!-- doc 294 -->
> Ordo cantilenarum in vespertinis precibus, et quidem in specie ad omnes dies pridianos
> festorum (vulgus vigilias appellat), hoc est: dominicas et festa proxime praecedentes dies.

The order of chants at the evening prayers, and that in particular for all the days before
feasts (the common people call them vigils), that is, the days next going before Sundays and
feasts.

**Lohr (county of Rieneck), *Vesperordnung*, 1588** (Sehling 11, p. 699):

<!-- doc 318 -->
> Die vesper belangend soll dieselbige sommerzeit umb 3 uhr, winterzeit aber der gelegenheit
> nach etwas früer nach anordnung der pfarherrn geleutet und angefangen werden und in vigiliis
> oder primis vesperis, das ist am abent der hohen und anderer festen, sonn- und feiertagen
> (damit die jugent im psalliren und choralgesang, wie in allen kirchen gebreuchlich, geüebet
> werde) mit lateinischen psalmen, responsoriis, hymnis, Magnificat, collectis, versiculis,
> Benedicamus und anderm nach ordnung der psalmodiae L. Lossii […] gesungen und gehalten werden

Touching vespers, the same shall be rung and begun in summer-time at three of the clock, but in
winter-time somewhat earlier as occasion serveth, after the ordering of the parsons; and *in
vigiliis* or at the first vespers, that is, on the eve of the high and other feasts, Sundays and
holy days (that the youth may be exercised in psalmody and plain-song, as is used in all
churches), it shall be sung and kept with Latin psalms, responds, hymns, *Magnificat*, collects,
versicles, *Benedicamus* and the rest, according to the order of the *Psalmodia* of L. Lossius
[…]

Burg has a sermon at vespers on the eve of the high feasts. **Burg, *Kirchen-Ordnung*, 1542**
(Sehling 2, p. 427):

<!-- doc 1257 -->
> Erstlich alle sonnabende und in vigiliis der hohen feste auch anderer evangelischer viertage
> sal in beiden pfarkirchen von dem pfarrer oder seinem diacon vesper gehalten werden […] Und so
> es vor not und biqueme eracht und angesehen und sonderlich in den vigilien der hohen feste, so
> sal nach der vesper auch geprediget werden

First, on all Saturdays, and *in vigiliis* of the high feasts and also of the other evangelical
holy days, vespers shall be held in both parish churches by the parson or his deacon […] And if
it be deemed needful and convenient, and in especial on the vigils of the high feasts, there
shall be preaching also after vespers.

Bremen gives a list of thirteen feasts, from Sunday to Michaelmas, and then adds a rule.
**Bremen, *Kirchenordnung*, 1561** (Sehling 7/2.2, p. 513):

<!-- doc 2221 -->
> 9. Dies Pentecostes una cum adiunctis duobus 10. Dies Trinitatis 11. Dies Johannis Bapistae
> 12. Dies Visitationis Mariae 13. Dies Michaelis In horum festorum vigilia et parasceve circa
> horam secundam fiat vespera a tota Latina Schola in templo Beatae Virginis canendo unum atque
> alterum psalmum Latinum, responsorium pro tempore et hymnum Magnificat. In reliquis autem
> templis Germanice canatur.

9. The day of Pentecost, together with the two joined to it. 10. The day of the Trinity. 11. The
   day of John the Baptist. 12. The day of the Visitation of Mary. 13. The day of Michael. On
   the vigil and preparation of these feasts, about the second hour, let vespers be held by the
   whole Latin school in the church of the Blessed Virgin, singing one or two Latin psalms, the
   respond for the season, and the hymn *Magnificat*. But in the rest of the churches let it be
   sung in German.

Nicolaus Hausmann's order for Anhalt is the only order in the corpus that would like to keep the
night vigil of Christmas and Easter. **Anhalt, Nicolaus Hausmann's order, 1532**
(Sehling 2, p. 542):

<!-- doc 1262 -->
> Vigilia paschce. natalis. Es wer auch nicht bose, das die christnacht und ostern mit der wache
> gehalden wurden, wie für alters.

The vigil of Easter; of the Nativity. It were also not evil that Christmas night and Easter were
kept with the watch, as of old.

**Note.** Hausmann goes on to name the abuses that have grown up around the night watch. He
proposes instead an early service at about three in the morning (p. 543).

---

## 5. The Lutheran scale of feasts

### 5.1 The common pattern

Most orders rank the feast days in the same few steps, though the words vary.

| Rank | Names used in the orders | Usual days | How kept |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sunday | *der Sontag*, *des Herren Tag*, *dies dominica* | every Sunday | the whole day; the norm by which the feasts are measured |
| 1. The high feasts | *hohe Feste*, *Hauptfeste*, *heuptfest des herrn Christi*, *de dre groten feste Christi*, *summa festa*, *Vierzeiten*, *große Feste* | Christmas, Easter, Whitsunday, each with one or two following days; often Michaelmas as the fourth; sometimes Ascension | the whole day, usually on two or three days; two or three sermons; Latin chant, sequence and proper preface; figural music; communion; no weddings |
| 2. The other feasts of Christ and "Gospel" feasts | *die andern festa Christi*, *fürnembste Feste*, *andere herliche fest*, *gemeine fest*, *mittelmeßige festa* | Circumcision (New Year), Epiphany, Purification, Annunciation, Ascension, Trinity; John the Baptist, Visitation, Michaelmas; in some orders Maundy Thursday and Good Friday | usually the whole day, "as on Sundays", with the introit, sequence and preface *de tempore*; in some orders only the forenoon |
| 3. Apostles' days and other saints' days | *Aposteltage*, *kleine Feste*, *halbe Feiertage*, *festa apostolorum* | the apostles; the Conversion of Paul; often Mary Magdalene, the Beheading of John, All Saints | a sermon in the forenoon, work after it; or no holy day, with the Gospel preached on the next Sunday or at a weekday sermon |
| 4. Abolished feasts | *papistische feste*, *abgötterische feste* | Corpus Christi, Holy Cross, the Conception, Nativity and Assumption of Mary (in most orders), All Souls, local saints | not kept; sometimes proclaimed and explained so that people know why they are gone |

The orders differ in three ways:

- **How many ranks they draw.** Some have two, many three, and a few four.
- **Where the middle feasts stand.** John the Baptist, the Visitation, Michaelmas, and in some
  orders Purification and Annunciation, sit either with the feasts of Christ or with the
  apostles.
- **What a lower rank means.** It may mean a half holy day, a single sermon, a transfer to
  Sunday, or the loss of the holy day altogether.

The families below show the main lines.

### 5.2 The Saxon and Mecklenburg family: "over and above these chiefest feasts"

The line starts with the Saxon *Unterricht der Visitatoren* of 1528. It speaks of two ranks in
its own words.

**Saxony (Ernestine), *Unterricht der Visitatoren*, 1528** (Sehling 1, p. 164):

<!-- doc 9 -->
> Were auch gut, das sie eintrechtiglich feierten, die sontage, annunciationis, purificationis,
> visitationis der reinen jungfrauen Maria, S. Johannis des teufers, Michaelis, der aposteln,
> Magdalene, die selben feste weren denn bereit abgangen, und künten nicht bequemlich alle wider
> aufgericht werden. Und in sonderheit sol man halten den christag, beschneidung, epiphanie, die
> osterfeier, auffart, pfingsten, doch abgethan, was unchristlich legenden oder gesang darinnen
> gefunden werden.

It were good also that they should keep holy day with one accord on the Sundays, the
Annunciation, the Purification, the Visitation of the pure Virgin Mary, St John the Baptist,
Michaelmas, the apostles', Magdalene; unless those feasts be already fallen away and could not
conveniently all be set up again. And in especial one shall keep Christmas, the Circumcision,
the Epiphany, the Easter holy days, the Ascension, Whitsuntide; yet with all things done away
that are found therein of unchristian legends or chants.

In Albertine Saxony the feasts of Christ follow the preamble quoted in §3.7. Sehling gives this
part of the order in his own summary but quotes the text. **Saxony (Albertine),
*Kirchenordnung*, 1539** (Sehling 1, p. 274):

<!-- doc 30 -->
> Der tag nativitatis oder der geburt Christi sampt den andern und auch dem dritten
> nechstfolgenden, so man daran communicanten hat. Der tag circumcisionis oder der beschneidung
> Christi. Der tag epiphanie, das ist der erscheinung oder offenbarung Christi, da die weisen
> aus morgenland durch erscheinung des sterns zu Christo kamen. Dieser tag wird auch gehalten
> von der taufe Christi. Der tag der offenbarung Christi in dem tempel, so man nennet
> purificationis Mariae. Der tag annuntiationis seu conceptionis Christi, da Christus in der
> jungfrauen leib empfangen ist. Der tag coenae domini, so man nennet den grünen donnerstag,
> daran von dem abendmal und hochwirdigen sakrament zu predigen ist. Der tag des leidens
> Christi, so der karfreitag genennet wird. Der ostertag von der auferstehung des herrn sampt
> dem nechstfolgenden und dritten, so man communicanten hat. Der tag der himelfart Christi. Der
> pfingstag von der sendung des heiligen geists. Der heiligen dreifaltigkeit fest gefellet auf
> den nehesten sontag nach pfingsten. Hierüber sollen auch diese fest gehalten werden. Der tag
> S. Johannis des teufers zu ehren dem heiligen predigampt des evangelii von Christo. Der tag
> visitationis, da Maria ire mume Elisabeth heimgesucht hat von wegen derselben historien des
> evangelii. Der tag Michaelis, daran von den heiligen engeln zu predigen. […] Man mag auch die
> tage der heiligen aposteln halten, das man vor mittag daran predige und, so es fürfelt, die
> communio halte. Nachmittage aber mag man wol der teglichen arbeit warten, oder, wo man wil,
> mag man die evangelia und gedechtnis von den heiligen aposteln auf die nehesten sontag, so
> darnach gefallen, zur mittags oder vesper predigt verlegen. Desgleichen mag es auch mit
> etlichen anderen festen der heiligen, welcher historien im evangelio beschrieben sind,
> gehalten werden, als da sind S. Paulus bekerung, Marie Magalena, S. Johannis enthauptung, S.
> Stephan etc.

The day of the Nativity or birth of Christ, with the second and also the third next following,
if there be communicants thereon. The day of the Circumcision of Christ. The day of the
Epiphany, that is, of the appearing or manifestation of Christ, when the wise men from the east
came to Christ by the appearing of the star; this day is also kept of the baptism of Christ. The
day of the manifestation of Christ in the temple, which is called the Purification of Mary. The
day of the Annunciation or Conception of Christ, when Christ was conceived in the Virgin's womb.
The day of the Lord's Supper, which is called Maundy Thursday, whereon one is to preach of the
Supper and the most worthy Sacrament. The day of the Passion of Christ, which is called Good
